Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one is a special and important person with a special mission or purpose.\n  - Reduced Sleep Duration: Sleeping significantly less than usual but feeling refreshed rather than tired.\n  - Loss of Sleep Need: Not feeling the urge to sleep or the thought that sleep is necessary.\n  - Indifference to Insomnia: Feeling no problem and continuing activities despite staying up for several nights.\n  - Racing Thoughts: Thoughts spinning too fast in the head, constantly jumping from one idea to another.\n  - Flooding of Thoughts: Too many thoughts emerging simultaneously, making them disorganized.\n  - Specific Plan for Suicide: Conceiving specific scenarios or methods regarding when, where, and how to die.\n  - Suicide Attempt: Actual actions taken to end one's life or preparatory behaviors like hoarding pills or writing a will.\n  Duration: 3 months\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - The presence of psychotic symptoms during a period of stable mood suggests that the condition might not be purely mood-driven, which is a characteristic that could align with Schizophrenia. Loss of Sleep Need: Not feeling the urge to sleep or the thought that sleep is necessary.\n  - Indifference to Insomnia: Feeling no problem and continuing activities despite staying up for several nights.\n  - Inability to Control Thoughts: Unable to stop or slow down thoughts and unable to calm the mind.\n  Duration: 1 month\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Recurrent Thoughts of Death: Passive wish to die (e.g., 'I wish I wouldn't wake up') without active intent to kill oneself.\n  - Suicidal Ideation: Recurring thoughts of 'I want to die' or 'I want to kill myself' without a specific plan.\n  - Suicidal Impulse: Strong, sudden urges to jump from a height or run into traffic without a concrete plan.\n  - Specific Plan for Suicide: Conceiving specific scenarios or methods regarding when, where, and how to die.\n  - Suicide Attempt: Actual actions taken to end one's life or preparatory behaviors like hoarding pills or writing a will.\n  Duration: 3 months\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 10 months\n  - Additional: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  - Additional: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  - Additional: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: Delusions\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n  - Derailment or Loose Associations: A speech pattern of switching rapidly from one topic to another with no logical connection between thoughts.\n  - Tangentiality: Speech that deviates entirely from the question and fails to answer directly.\n  - Incoherence or Word Salad: Severe fragmentation of speech and sentence structure, making it nearly impossible to understand.\n  - Neologisms: Using made-up words that have meaning only to the individual.\n  - Clang Associations: Speech governed by sounds, such as rhyming, rather than the meaning of words.\n  Duration: 5 months\n▸ manic_episode\n  - Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Inflated Self-Confidence: Feeling capable of anything and believing that failure is impossible, unlike one's usual self.\n  - Overestimation of Abilities: Unrealistically assessing one's talents, knowledge, or abilities as superior.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one is a special and important person with a special mission or purpose.\n  - Reduced Sleep Duration: Sleeping significantly less than usual but feeling refreshed rather than tired.\n  - Loss of Sleep Need: Not feeling the urge to sleep or the thought that sleep is necessary.\n  - Indifference to Insomnia: Feeling no problem and continuing activities despite staying up for several nights.\n  - Racing Thoughts: Thoughts spinning too fast in the head, constantly jumping from one idea to another.\n  - Flooding of Thoughts: Too many thoughts emerging simultaneously, making them disorganized.\n  - Inability to Control Thoughts: Unable to stop or slow down thoughts and unable to calm the mind.\n  Duration: 1 month\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Recurrent Thoughts of Death: Passive wish to die (e.g., 'I wish I wouldn't wake up') without active intent to kill oneself.\n  - Suicidal Ideation: Recurring thoughts of 'I want to die' or 'I want to kill myself' without a specific plan.\n  - Suicidal Impulse: Strong, sudden urges to jump from a height or run into traffic without a concrete plan.\n  - Specific Plan for Suicide: Conceiving specific scenarios or methods regarding when, where, and how to die.\n  - Suicide Attempt: Actual actions taken to end one's life or preparatory behaviors like hoarding pills or writing a will.\n  Duration: 3 months\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Erotomanic Delusions: The false belief that another person is in love with or has affection for them.\n  - Nihilistic Delusions: A destructive or negative conviction that the world is ending or that a major catastrophe will occur.\n  - Somatic Delusions: The baseless conviction that there is a serious abnormality with one's body or organs.\n  - Jealous Delusions: The conviction that one's spouse or romantic partner is being unfaithful without sufficient evidence.\n  - Auditory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of hearing voices or sounds when there is no actual sound.\n  - Visual Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of seeing people or objects that do not exist.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n  - Derailment or Loose Associations: A speech pattern of switching rapidly from one topic to another with no logical connection between thoughts.\n  - Tangentiality: Speech that deviates entirely from the question and fails to answer directly.\n  - Incoherence or Word Salad: Severe fragmentation of speech and sentence structure, making it nearly impossible to understand.\n  - Neologisms: Using made-up words that have meaning only to the individual.\n  - Clang Associations: Speech governed by sounds, such as rhyming, rather than the meaning of words.\n  Duration: 5 months\n▸ manic_episode\n  - Elevated Mood: A mood that is abnormally high, euphoric, and full of unmotivated happiness and vitality.\n  - Irritable Mood: A state where one gets angry or aggressive even at minor stimuli.\n  - Mood Lability: Rapid shifts in mood, alternating unpredictably between euphoria and irritability.\n  - Increased Energy: Overflowing physical and mental energy, leading to an inability to stay still and a desire to keep moving.\n  - Hyperactivity: Doing far more work than usual or starting multiple activities simultaneously.\n  - Psychomotor Agitation: Inability to tolerate resting or stopping, driven by a constant need to do something.\n  - Inflated Self-Confidence: Feeling capable of anything and believing that failure is impossible, unlike one's usual self.\n  - Overestimation of Abilities: Unrealistically assessing one's talents, knowledge, or abilities as superior.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one is a special and important person with a special mission or purpose.\n  - Reduced Sleep Duration: Sleeping significantly less than usual but feeling refreshed rather than tired.\n  - Loss of Sleep Need: Not feeling the urge to sleep or the thought that sleep is necessary.\n  - Indifference to Insomnia: Feeling no problem and continuing activities despite staying up for several nights.\n  - Racing Thoughts: Thoughts spinning too fast in the head, constantly jumping from one idea to another.\n  - Flooding of Thoughts: Too many thoughts emerging simultaneously, making them disorganized.\n  - Inability to Control Thoughts: Unable to stop or slow down thoughts and unable to calm the mind.\n  Duration: 1 month\n▸ delusions_and_hallucinations\n  - Persecutory Delusions: The false belief that one is being harmed or monitored by other people or organizations.\n  - Referential Delusions: The conviction that ordinary events, remarks, or behaviors have specific relevance to oneself.\n  - Grandiose Delusions: The false belief that one possesses special abilities, power, or fame.\n  - Tactile Hallucinations: Experiencing the sensation of being touched or movement on the skin when there is no actual stimulus.\n  - Olfactory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of smelling odors that do not exist.\n  - Gustatory Hallucinations: The perceptual experience of tasting flavors that are not actually present.\n▸ depressive_episode\n  - Depressed Mood: A persistently sunken mood or feeling of depression.\n  - Tearfulness: A state of frequent crying or inability to hold back tears.\n  - Sadness: A state where a sad or gloomy mood is maintained continuously.\n  - Emptiness: A feeling of inner emptiness or a state of feeling no emotion.\n  - Emotional Numbness: A state where emotions feel dulled or paralyzed.\n  - Recurrent Thoughts of Death: Passive wish to die (e.g., 'I wish I wouldn't wake up') without active intent to kill oneself.\n  - Suicidal Ideation: Recurring thoughts of 'I want to die' or 'I want to kill myself' without a specific plan.\n  - Suicidal Impulse: Strong, sudden urges to jump from a height or run into traffic without a concrete plan.\n  - Specific Plan for Suicide: Conceiving specific scenarios or methods regarding when, where, and how to die.\n  - Suicide Attempt: Actual actions taken to end one's life or preparatory behaviors like hoarding pills or writing a will.\n  Duration: 3 months\n▸ Critical Diagnostic Criteria\n  - Duration: 10 months\n  - Additional: psychotic_symptoms and manic_episode occur concurrently\n  - Additional: manic_episode is present for the majority of the total duration of the active and residual portions of the illness\n  - Additional: delusions_and_hallucinations occur for 2 or more weeks in the absence of a major mood episode\n\nRules:\n- If the patient can specifically answer based on the profile, set matched=true, list related\n  sections/items in matched_sections, and write a short English strategy in answer_strategy\n  on what tone and content the patient should reply with.\n- If the question is not in the profile or can only be inferred, set matched=false and instruct\n  in answer_strategy to respond briefly (e.g., \"I'm not sure\", \"I haven't thought about it\").\n- If patient_turn_index is 1 (patient's first reply), ONLY focus the strategy on the chief complaint\n  (do NOT reveal other symptoms).\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"matched\": true|false, \"matched_sections\": [\"...\"], \"answer_strategy\": \"...\"}\n"

I'm not really sure of the exact impact it's had on my work or relationships, but it just feels like a lot to handle.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how long you've been experiencing these voices and feelings of being watched without any significant mood symptoms, such as depression or mania, and whether this period is substantial enough to consider these psychotic symptoms as a dominant feature of your illness?\n\nPatient: Thinking back, I would say there was a period of about two weeks, maybe slightly more, where I still heard the voices and felt watched without feeling depressed or high. It's hard to say if it's the dominant part of my illness, but those couple of weeks on their own were definitely noticeable and difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices and feeling of being watched have been present for a substantial amount of time, even when your mood symptoms were not prominent, to the point where they could be considered a major part of your condition, rather than just a secondary symptom to your mood swings?\n\nPatient: Yes, I would say they are a major, independent part of my struggles because they've been going on for about five months in total.     "role": "system",

    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I'm here because I've been hearing strange voices and constantly feeling like I'm being monitored or watched by other people. This has been going on for about five months now, and it's getting very hard to live with.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as periods of extreme sadness, hopelessness, or elevated mood, irritability, or increased energy, alongside these voices and feelings of being watched?\n\nPatient: Yes, I've gone through both extremes. I had about three months where I felt deeply depressed and empty, but I also had a month where my energy was incredibly high and I felt really irritable. That high-energy period actually happened at the exact same time I was hearing the voices and feeling like I was being watched.\n\nDoctor: For how much of the time that you've been experiencing these voices and feelings of being watched have you also been experiencing either the depressive symptoms or the high-energy irritability, and were there any periods where the voices and being watched feelings occurred without either of these mood states?\n\nPatient: Most of the time I've been hearing the voices, I was also dealing with either the depression or that high-energy phase. But there was definitely a period of about a month where I still felt monitored and heard the voices, even though my mood had leveled out and I wasn't depressed or manic.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about that month when your mood was stable, yet you still experienced the voices and feeling of being watched - did these psychotic symptoms dominate your life during that time, or were they less prominent compared to when you were experiencing mood swings?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure how to compare the intensity of the symptoms between those times. However, the voices and the constant feeling of being watched were definitely still there during that stable month.\n\nDoctor: During that month when your mood was stable and you were still experiencing the voices and feeling of being watched, were you able to maintain your daily routines, work, or social relationships, or did these psychotic symptoms significantly interfere with your functioning?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure, to be honest. It's difficult to recall exactly how much those feelings and voices interfered with my work or daily routine during that specific month.\n\nDoctor: Let's try to focus on your overall experience: have the voices and feelings of being watched, regardless of your mood, caused significant problems in your daily life, such as affecting your ability to work, maintain relationships, or take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: It has been very difficult to manage everything lately with all of this going on. I'm not really sure of the exact impact it's had on my work or relationships, but it just feels like a lot to handle.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how long you've been experiencing these voices and feelings of being watched without any significant mood symptoms, such as depression or mania, and whether this period is substantial enough to consider these psychotic symptoms as a dominant feature of your illness?\n\nPatient: Thinking back, I would say there was a period of about two weeks, maybe slightly more, where I still heard the voices and felt watched without feeling depressed or high. It's hard to say if it's the dominant part of my illness, but those couple of weeks on their own were definitely noticeable and difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices and feeling of being watched have been present for a substantial amount of time, even when your mood symptoms were not prominent, to the point where they could be considered a major part of your condition, rather than just a secondary symptom to your mood swings?\n\nPatient: Yes, I would say they are a major, independent part of my struggles because they've been going on for about five months in total. Even during that period of at least two weeks when my mood was completely stable, the voices and the feeling of being watched were still very clear. It felt like they were happening completely on their own, separate from my mood swings.\n\nDoctor: So, to confirm, you've experienced a mix of depressive and manic episodes, but you've also had periods where the voices and feeling of being watched occurred without these mood symptoms, suggesting that these psychotic symptoms are a significant and independent part of your condition - is that a fair summary of your experience?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's a very fair summary of what I've been going through. I've experienced both the deep depression and the high-energy manic phases, but there were definitely times when my mood felt stable and I still heard the voices and felt watched. It really seems like those experiences happen on their own, completely independent of how happy or sad I am.\n\nDoctor: Based on what you've shared, it sounds like you've had a significant amount of time with psychotic symptoms, such as hearing voices and feeling watched, that have occurred both with and without mood episodes, which is consistent with a diagnosis of Schizoaffective Disorder; do you have any questions about this diagnosis or would you like to discuss treatment options?"

That high-energy period actually happened at the exact same time I was hearing the voices and feeling like I was being watched.\n\nDoctor: For how much of the time that you've been experiencing these voices and feelings of being watched have you also been experiencing either the depressive symptoms or the high-energy irritability, and were there any periods where the voices and being watched feelings occurred without either of these mood states?\n\nPatient: Most of the time I've been hearing the voices, I was also dealing with either the depression or that high-energy phase. But there was definitely a period of about a month where I still felt monitored and heard the voices, even though my mood had leveled out and I wasn't depressed or manic.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me more about that month when your mood was stable, yet you still experienced the voices and feeling of being watched - did these psychotic symptoms dominate your life during that time, or were they less prominent compared to when you were experiencing mood swings?\n\nPatient: I'm not entirely sure how to compare the intensity of the symptoms between those times. However, the voices and the constant feeling of being watched were definitely still there during that stable month.\n\nDoctor: During that month when your mood was stable and you were still experiencing the voices and feeling of being watched, were you able to maintain your daily routines, work, or social relationships, or did these psychotic symptoms significantly interfere with your functioning?\n\nPatient: I'm not quite sure, to be honest. It's difficult to recall exactly how much those feelings and voices interfered with my work or daily routine during that specific month.\n\nDoctor: Let's try to focus on your overall experience: have the voices and feelings of being watched, regardless of your mood, caused significant problems in your daily life, such as affecting your ability to work, maintain relationships, or take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: It has been very difficult to manage everything lately with all of this going on. I'm not really sure of the exact impact it's had on my work or relationships, but it just feels like a lot to handle.\n\nDoctor: Can you estimate how long you've been experiencing these voices and feelings of being watched without any significant mood symptoms, such as depression or mania, and whether this period is substantial enough to consider these psychotic symptoms as a dominant feature of your illness?\n\nPatient: Thinking back, I would say there was a period of about two weeks, maybe slightly more, where I still heard the voices and felt watched without feeling depressed or high. It's hard to say if it's the dominant part of my illness, but those couple of weeks on their own were definitely noticeable and difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voices and feeling of being watched have been present for a substantial amount of time, even when your mood symptoms were not prominent, to the point where they could be considered a major part of your condition, rather than just a secondary symptom to your mood swings?\n\nPatient: Yes, I would say they are a major, independent part of my struggles because they've been going on for about five months in total. Even during that period of at least two weeks when my mood was completely stable, the voices and the feeling of being watched were still very clear. It felt like they were happening completely on their own, separate from my mood swings.\n\nDoctor: So, to confirm, you've experienced a mix of depressive and manic episodes, but you've also had periods where the voices and feeling of being watched occurred without these mood symptoms, suggesting that these psychotic symptoms are a significant and independent part of your condition - is that a fair summary of your experience?\n\nPatient: Yes, that's a very fair summary of what I've been going through. I've experienced both the deep depression and the high-energy manic phases, but there were definitely times when my mood felt stable and I still heard the voices and felt watched. It really seems like those experiences happen on their own, completely independent of how happy or sad I am.\n\nDoctor: Based on what you've shared, it sounds like you've had a significant amount of time with psychotic symptoms, such as hearing voices and feeling watched, that have occurred both with and without mood episodes, which is consistent with a diagnosis of Schizoaffective Disorder; do you have any questions about this diagnosis or would you like to discuss treatment options?\n\nPatient: Hearing that diagnosis is a bit overwhelming, but it is also a relief to finally have a name for what has been happening to me. What do the treatment options for this usually look like, and what are our next steps?"
  }

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol). Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.
